3

Is International Law Fading Away in State Practice on Cyber Operations?

François Delerue

INTERNATIONAL LAW APPLIES to cyberspace and cyber operations. In spite of its centrality across international debates on cyber norms, international law lacks concrete applications in state practices, or remains at best referred to in abstract terms. In the last decade, a number of states has developed the practice of public attribution of cyber operations to other states. In all past examples, however, the vast majority of the attributing states made no reference to international law and only a few made loose references to ‘international law’ or the ‘international rules-based order’. To date, no attributing statement has ever clearly characterized which rule or principle of international law has been breached, nor referred to the categories of the international legal framework to attribute and react to these acts. In order to entangle this phenomenon, this chapter explores why states are not using the rules and principles of international law in their practice while promoting them in international discussions. This chapter tests four hypotheses that may explain the absence of international law: (1) Because the international legal framework remains unsettled?; (2) Are the references to international law implicit?; (3) Is it because international law is not breached by the condemned behaviours?; (4) Are states reluctant to use international law to avoid its further development?

International Law in State Practice in Cyberspace

On 17 April 2020, the US Department of State issued a statement condemning malicious cyber activities taking advantage of the coronavirus crisis and reaffirming that the United States ‘promotes a framework of responsible state behaviour in cyberspace, including non-binding norms,’ but without making any reference to international law (Pompeo 2020a). Similarly, on 1 September 2020, the United States Embassy in Georgia issued a statement condemning an ‘irresponsible attack attempted to illegally access pandemic management information stored in the databases of the [Georgian Ministry of Health] and its units’ (US Embassy in Georgia 2020), reaffirming again the importance of the framework of responsible state behaviour in cyberspace without making any reference to international law. These examples illustrate a general trend in international relations, in particular in the practice of states in cyberspace: rules and principles of international law tends to fade away in favour of non-binding norms of responsible state behaviour in cyberspace.

The Centrality of International Law in International Discussions and the Discourse of States

International law, and in particular the Charter of the United Nations, is the backbone of contemporary international relations and remains crucial in maintaining international peace and security. The applicability of international law to cyberspace is nowadays consensual among states and other actors: international law applies to cyberspace and cyber operations.1 This has notably been affirmed by the consensual reports of the UN Group of Governmental Experts (GGE) on Developments in the Field of Information and Telecommunications in the Context of International Security in 2013 and 2015, which noted that:

International law, and in particular the Charter of the United Nations, is applicable and is essential to maintaining peace and stability and promoting an open, secure, peaceful and accessible ICT environment (UN General Assembly 2013, 8, para 19; 2019, 12, para 24).

Yet, international law continues to be a key focus of ongoing international negotiations and academic debates. The question of the applicability of international law being settled, the debate has moved on to the question of how the rules and principles of international law are to be applied to cyberspace. At the multilateral level, the effort to clarify the interpretation of the rules and principles of international law has already been initiated by the successive GGE since 2012 (third GGE) and the Open-Ended Working Group (OEWG) on Developments in the Field of Information and Telecommunications in the Context of International Security (Adamson 2020, 25). Moreover, a growing number of states publicize their approach on the rules and principles of international law applicable to cyberspace.2 Two important caveats must be addressed, though. On the one hand, less than a dozen states have made their interpretation public. On the other hand, the vast majority of the detailed approaches now publicized have been released by Western states. Therefore, the picture we have is geographically limited and partial. This second limitation may, however, recede in the future for two reasons. First, the recent publication by Iran of its approach may actually incentivize other non-Western states to follow suit (Iran 2020). It is indeed the first time that a non-Western state has publicly exposed a detailed approach on this matter. Second, UNGA Resolution 73/266 requested that the states participating in the GGE submit their views on how international law should be applied to cyberspace. As such, there is a growing push for the states not taking part in the GGE to disclose their views as well, notably within the framework of the OEWG.

These different efforts to clarify the interpretation of the rules and principles of international law in cyberspace lead to two important observations for this study. First, there is no contestation of the international legal framework applicable to cyber operations. Indeed, neither states nor scholars are today contesting the applicability of branches of international law in cyberspace.3 Moreover, the different positions expressed by states, either as part of the UN-led discussions or unilaterally, tend to show a certain level of convergence among states on the interpretation of the different branches of international law in this specific context. Second, international law remains a key element in states’ rhetoric and international discussions on the peace and stability of cyberspace.

Identifying the Absence of International Law in State Practice

Despite the centrality of international law in the international discussions, when it comes to state practice, international law seems to be in fact rather absent. In past years, for instance, some states have developed a practice of public attribution of cyber operations to other states. Yet, it must be noted that these public attributions have only been done by a limited number of states, often Western countries and notably by the members of the Five Eyes alliance (Australia, Canada, New Zealand, United Kingdom and the United States) as well as Estonia and the Netherlands. Thus, the picture on this practice is geographically limited, leading Florian J. Egloff to say that we have a ‘skewed picture’ of public attribution (Egloff 2020, 61).

In all past examples, the vast majority of the attributing states made no reference to international law and only a small number made vague references to ‘international law’ or the ‘international rules-based order’. To date, no attributing statement has ever clearly characterized which rule or principle of international law has been breached nor referred to the categories of the international legal framework to attribute and react to these acts.

The coordinated attribution of February 2020 offers a good illustration to the current state practice. On 20 February 2020, twenty-one states and the European Union4 issued statements condemning cyber operations against Georgia and a few of them went further in attributing them to Russia (Nakashidze 2020; Roguski 2020b). In addition to Georgia, thirteen states attributed the cyber operations to either Russia or the GRU (the Main Directorate of the General Staff of the Armed Forces of the Russian Federation), namely Australia, Canada, Denmark, Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Montenegro, The Netherlands, New Zealand, Poland, Ukraine, United Kingdom, and the United States.5 The statements issued by Austria,6 Czech Republic,7 Guam,8 Iceland,9 Norway,10 Romania (2020), Sweden11 and the European Union (2020) condemned the cyber operations without attributing them.

This sample includes twenty-two statements taking different forms, from a statement issued by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs to a tweet on the account of the Minister of Foreign Affairs. Only eleven statements out of twenty-two made an explicit reference to international law or the international rules-based order (i.e., the statements issued by Austria, Canada, Czech Republic, Estonia, European Union, Latvia, Montenegro, the Netherlands, Romania, Ukraine and United Kingdom). Norms of responsible behaviour have been mentioned in seven statements, five of which also referred to international law. Interestingly, six statements refer to a ‘framework of responsible state behaviour in cyberspace’, without further clarification. As highlighted by the Australian statement (2020), such reference was made and clarified in the Joint Statement on Advancing Responsible State Behaviour in Cyberspace adopted by several states in September 2019, which noted that:

UN member states have increasingly coalesced around an evolving framework of responsible state behavior in cyberspace (framework), which supports the international rules-based order, affirms the applicability of international law to state-on-state behavior, adherence to voluntary norms of responsible state behavior in peacetime, and the development and implementation of practical confidence building measures to help reduce the risk of conflict stemming from cyber incidents. All members of the United Nations General Assembly have repeatedly affirmed this framework, articulated in three successive UN Groups of Governmental Experts reports in 2010, 2013, and 2015. (‘Joint Statement’ 2019)

The Final Substantive Report of the OEWG released on 10 March 2021 also reaffirmed that the framework of responsible state behaviour in cyberspace includes both international law and norms of responsible behaviour (OEWG 2021, 3 para. 17). It should be noted, however, that in the recent US Department of State’s statement condemning malicious cyber activities taking advantage of the COVID-19 crisis, it is written that the United States ‘promotes a framework of responsible state behaviour in cyberspace, including non-binding norms’ (Pompeo 2020b). One may wonder why such an emphasis is placed on norms and not on international law.

In sum, only two-thirds of the statements made a reference, more or less explicit, to international law. Yet, none of these statements go further on the application of the rules or principles of international law to the concerned cyber operations. On the attribution of the cyber operations to Russia, some statements explicitly mention Russia or just the GRU, but none of them develop on which legal ground they impute these behaviours to Russia. Yet, I concede that such a clarification may be considered unnecessary since it is not a requirement of international law, nor it would change the attribution.

On the lawfulness of the behaviour, Georgia states that these cyber operations are ‘infringing Georgia’s sovereignty in order to hinder the country’s European and Euro-Atlantic integration and democratic development’. Yet, it must be noted that the mention here seems to refer to sovereignty as a political concept rather than a rule or principle of international law. Czech Republic referred to ‘a clear violation of state sovereignty’, which seems to refer to a violation of international law. Additionally, seven other states (Lithuania, Montenegro, The Netherlands, Poland, Romania, Ukraine and United Kingdom) made similar references to ‘infringement’ or ‘undermining’ sovereignty and territorial integrity, without further clarification on whether they referred to sovereignty as a political concept or a rule of international law. Similarly, New Zealand stated that these cyber operations ‘were designed to interfere in Georgia’s political and economic freedom’, which may be a reference to the prohibition of intervention. Yet, New Zealand did not explicitly refer to this prohibition nor explicitly qualified the behaviour as an unlawful intervention. Estonia stated that ‘[a]s a result of practicing unlawful cyber operations, Russia should be held accountable according to existing international norms and treaties’, without explaining whether this was a general comment or a specific affirmation on the lawfulness of the cyber operations against Georgia. Estonia did not develop which rule or principle of international law has been breached in this case.

On the potential consequences and reactions, none of the statements make reference to either the legal responsibility of Russia nor to either adopted or contemplated unilateral measures (self-defence, countermeasures or measures or retorsion) in reaction to these cyber operations. This is rather logical, since they did not take position on the wrongfulness of the behaviours. In sum, the example of the coordinated attributions of 20 February 2020 shows that despite some general and abstract references, the rules and principles of international law are generally not referred to nor applied in practice. In other words, international law seems to be absent or perceived as an abstract concept without concrete application in state practice regarding cyber operations. The next section of this chapter will question the rationale behind the lack of use of international law in state practice on the attribution of cyber operations. The example of the coordinated attribution of February 2020 illustrates how international laws tend to be rather absent in state practice. The section will also discuss the different forms of state practice in cyberspace and identify the relevant dimensions for this study.

Navigating State Practice

The development of the practice of state regarding cyber operations revolves around two different categories: (1) when states conduct or sponsor cyber operations; (2) when states are reacting to cyber operations conducted or sponsored by other states, either because they are the victims of these acts or because they react in support to the victims. Conducting and sponsoring cyber operations today is an established practice for numerous states, with very different modus operandi and motives. Yet, it may be observed that there is very little, if any, publicly available data on their approach to international law in the perpetration of actual cyber operations. Two main reasons may explain this.

On the one hand, the applicability of international law is no longer a matter of contestation, and an increasing number of states have publicly exposed their approach to the rules and principles of international law applicable to cyber operations. Some armies even went one step further by including these in their military manuals.12 Thus, in theory, cyber operations conducted by states, or on their behalf, should be conducted in a manner complying with the existing international legal framework. In practice, however, it is today impossible to assess whether states are acting in compliance with international law. States do not openly communicate their actual practice of conducting or sponsoring cyber operations, including on the lawfulness of these actions. On the other hand, some cyber operations that are allegedly attributed to one or more states are manifestly violating specific rules or principles of international law. Stuxnet, for instance, is generally considered by scholars to constitute an example of a use of force in cyberspace (Schmitt and Vihul 2017, 342; Delerue 2020, 310, 500–501). In such cases, none of the sponsoring states has ever acknowledged its responsibility resulting from an internationally wrongful act nor justified its conduct by invoking a circumstance that would excuse or preclude its wrongfulness.13

When assessing the lawfulness of the different examples of cyber operations that have been publicly discussed in the past years, it appears that the majority may be considered as internationally wrongful acts. Indeed, from a theoretical point of view, the majority met the criteria to constitute a violation of the territorial sovereignty of the targeted states, while some of them may be seen as unlawful intervention or even unlawful recourse to force in the case of Stuxnet. Building on this observation, one may argue that state practice, at least for some states, seems to involve a certain level of contestation of the existing legal framework as these states conduct or sponsor wrongful cyber operations. However, violation and contestation of rules and principles of international law must be distinguished, as I have argued elsewhere, drawing from the jurisprudence of the International Court of Justice (ICJ) (Delerue 2019a, 309–310). Indeed, in the Nicaragua case, the ICJ stated that:

If a State acts in a way prima facie incompatible with a recognized rule, but defends its conduct by appealing to exceptions or justifications contained within the rule itself, then whether or not the State’s conduct is in fact justifiable on that basis, the significance of that attitude is to confirm rather than to weaken the rule. (‘Military and Paramilitary Activities’ 1986, para 186)

Despite a growing number of public attributions, no state has ever acknowledged its paternity, nor defended itself for doing it. For this reason, it cannot be concluded whether such defence would be based on international law or, rather, challenge it. Yet, I believe it is likely that states would defend such behaviour using the exceptions and justifications offered by international law rather than contesting its applicability. To sum up the first category, it is impossible to assess the extent to which state conduct or sponsoring of cyber operations take into account international law, due to a lack of publicly available data. In regard to the second category, more data is available on how states are reacting to cyber operations, and this is why it is the main focus of this chapter.

The second category comprises the practice of states reacting to cyber operations conducted or sponsored by other states, either because they are the victims of these acts or because they react in support of the victims. From the point of view of international law, a state must follow a specific legal reasoning before being able to adopt unilateral measures against the wrongdoing actor (Delerue 2019b). When a state-conducted or state-sponsored cyber operation occurs, it must be first attributed in order to determine whether a state is responsible and thus whether international law is the relevant legal framework. The second step is to assess its wrongfulness. Third, the wrongdoing state bears an obligation of cessation and non-repetition of the wrongful act, as well as an obligation of reparation for the caused injury. The injured state may be entitled to invoke the responsibility of the sponsoring state and to seek reparations for the caused damage. Fourth, the state victim of an unlawful cyber operation may have recourse to extrajudicial measures to compel the wrongdoing state to fulfil its obligations, namely measures of retorsion, countermeasures and self-defence.

In terms of publicly documented practice, however, there are only two actions that have been publicly acknowledged. First, and mainly, is public attribution of cyber operations to another state. Second, attribution, indictments and adoption of sanctions against individuals and entities involved in the perpetration of state-sponsored cyber operations. The next section of this chapter focuses on these practices by states.

Navigating State Practice on Public Attribution and International Law

Over the past years, some states have developed a practice of ‘naming and shaming’ the responsible actors for their cyber operations. Yet, this constitutes only one form of the developing practice of states to attribute, openly or indirectly, cyber operations to other states and actors.14 While analysing state practice on attribution, it is thus necessary to distinguish situations featuring an actual attribution to a state with the aim to invoke its responsibility from those featuring only an incidental attribution, and those lacking an attribution whatsoever.

In order to navigate and illustrate these different dimensions of states practice, I will build on the different mechanisms of attribution described by Kirsten E. Eichensehr in her recent article: ‘attribution-by-indictment’, ‘attribution-by-sanctions’, ‘attribution-by-alert’ and ‘attribution-by-press-release’ (Eichensehr 2020, 533–536). Two remarks on these different mechanisms: First, these mechanisms are not exclusive of each other since they have different purposes, and thus they are sometimes used in conjunction (Eichensehr 2020, 536). Second, only the fourth mechanism, ‘attribution-by-press release’, is actually aimed at attributing a cyber operation to a state and, potentially, invoking that state’s responsibility. This is thus only the fourth mechanism that is relevant in studying how states are using international law in their practice.

In the three other mechanisms of attribution, a responsible state may be named even if it is not always the case, as primary objective of these mechanisms is not directed toward that state nor to produce international legal consequences between the two concerned states. For this reason, they usually do not rely on invocation and implementation of rules or principles of international law. This is why these three mechanisms are less relevant for the present study. Yet, they may still produce incidental consequences within the international legal framework. In an attribution-by-indictment, the objective is to assess whether individuals and entities have violated domestic law in performing cyber operations. In the case of attribution-by-sanctions, the objective is to adopt restrictive measures against individuals or entities involved in cyber operations. In both cases, the concerned individuals or entities are targeted by the criminal proceeding or the sanctions because of their involvement in a state-sponsored cyber operation, often because they are either agents or organs of that state. In both cases, however, even if the concerned state is explicitly mentioned, neither the indictments nor the sanctions are directly aimed against that state from a legal point of view and, thus, the objective is not to create legal consequences on the responsible state but rather on the responsible individuals or entities. In the case of an attribution-by-alert, the alert is issued to warn victims or potential targets of a cyber threat. A state may be mentioned as sponsoring a specific type of cyber threat,15 but here also the objective of the act is not to produce legal consequence on the responsible state. As it has been argued, attribution of cyber operations to a state may take various forms, but only attribution-by-press-release is relevant to analyse state practice on the international law applicable to cyber operations. The remaining of this section will thus focus on that specific mechanism of attribution.

Four observations must be made on development and evolution of state practice in issuing statements, of various forms, to publicly attribute cyber operations to other states. First, this practice of issuing a public statement to attribute a cyber operation to another state was first developed by the United States, before being followed in suit by other states (Eichensehr 2020, 529–530). Indeed, in some early cases, such as the 2007 series of distributed denial-of-service (DDoS) attacks against Estonia, some declarations by state officials pointed out other states, but the victim states never officially and publicly attributed the cyber operations (Tikk, Kaska and Vihul 2010, 23–24; Delerue 2020, 84, 146–147). The 2014 hack of Sony Picture Entertainment is one of the first cases of public attribution by the United States (Sanger and Perlroth 2014), with a statement by the US Secretary of Homeland Security Jeh Johnson issued on 19 December 2014 (United States 2014).

Secondly, these public attributions are predominantly, and by far, done by the members of the Five Eyes alliance and some other Western states (Egloff 2020).

Third, there has been an important evolution in state practice. Public attributions were initially isolated acts before the development of coordinated and joint public attribution. WannaCry and NotPetya were the first cases of coordinated attribution (Delerue 2020, 165–177). A possible explanation is the fact that these two cases were not affecting just one state and that most of the attributing states were actually also the victims of this malware.16 It must be highlighted that this practice has continued and developed further, with an increasing number of attributions made by states that were not victims of the concerned cyber operations. On 4 October 2018, the United Kingdom and The Netherlands made the first joint attribution of cyber operations, via a joint statement by Prime Ministers Theresa May and Mark Rutte attributing several acts, and not only cyber operations, to the Russian military intelligence service (The Netherlands 2018; United Kingdom 2018a).

Fourth, and most importantly for this study, it must be observed that international law is generally absent from these statements, or only referred to in an abstract manner (Broeders, De Busser and Pawlak 2020, 8). This observation leads to the question developed in the next section.

Why Is International Law Absent in State Practice on Cyber Operations?

This section of the chapter develops some hypotheses on the rationales behind the lack of reference to and application of international law in state practice on the attribution of cyber operations.

A first hypothesis refers to the fact that states may still consider cyberspace as a ‘grey zone’ in which the application of the rules and principles of international law would still be subject to contestation. This could explain, at least partly, why some states just refer to norms of responsible behaviour, which may appear more concrete and tailor made for the cyber context. However, this hypothesis must be discarded for three reasons. First, the applicability of international law is not contested by states nor by scholars. Second, although the concrete interpretation of the rules and principles of international law in cyberspace is still a matter of discussion, a growing number of states have publicly outlined their interpretation of the international law applicable to cyberspace. In addition, academics and experts have produced important literature17 on that matter and there is an important effort to build capacity on legal matters related to cyberspace (Hollis, Rakhlina-Powsner and Villa 2020). Third, the main states in attributing cyber operations are also those that have publicly released detailed statements on their approach to international law in cyberspace. Indeed, countries like Australia, the Netherlands, United Kingdom and the United States18 have made clear their approach to the rules and principles of international law in cyberspace but never applied them in their various attributions.

This is also the case with branches of international law that have been extensively discussed, such as on the international legal framework applicable to cyber activities taking advantage of the COVID-19 pandemic. This matter was notably discussed during two Arria-formula meetings of the United Nations Security Council. The first one was held on 22 May 2020 and focused on ‘Cyber Stability, Conflict Prevention and Capacity Building’, organized by Estonia in cooperation with Belgium, the Dominican Republic, Indonesia and Kenya (‘Arria-formula meeting’ 2020a). The second one took place on 26 August 2020 and focused on ‘Cyber-Attacks Against Critical Infrastructure’, organized by Indonesia, in cooperation with Belgium, Estonia and Vietnam, and the International Committee of the Red Cross (ICRC) (‘Arria-formula meeting’ 2020b). In preparation of these meetings, numerous international lawyers have signed two statements restating the rules and principles of international law applicable in such circumstances (‘The Oxford Statement’ 2020; ‘The Second Oxford Statement’ 2020).

Turning to state practice on the matter, such as the two statements by the United States condemning such behaviours issued in April 202019 and in September 2020,20 both reaffirmed the importance of the framework of responsible state behaviour in cyberspace without making any reference to international law nor applying its rules or principles to the condemned cyber operations. This appears even more surprising since the US representative at the second Arria-formula meeting again made a statement condemning such behaviour as was made at the first Arria-formula meeting (US Mission to the UN 2020a), but adding that:

The United States has been, and will continue to be, a leader in these efforts and continues to advocate for states to implement and adhere to the GGE framework, which consists of the application of relevant international law to state activities in cyberspace, voluntary non-binding norms of state behaviour applicable in peacetime, and the implementation of practical Confidence Building Measures. The United States joined the unanimous commitment in GA Resolution 70/237 to have this framework guide states’ use of ICTs. (US Mission to the UN 2020b)

Here we have an example of this schizophrenic practice of some states which, on the one hand, promote international law in their public declaration yet, on the other, do not use nor operationalize it in their practice.

A second hypothesis could be that the applicability and application of international law are taken for granted and, thus, the references are implicit. As developed earlier, the framework of responsible state behaviour sometimes referred to by states includes both international law and norms of responsible behaviour. Yet, this would only cover a lack of general reference to international law but not the absence of assessment of the wrongfulness of the concerned behaviours.

A third hypothesis could be that the concerned behaviours are not breaching international law, at least from the point of view of the attributing states. Contrary to the two previous hypotheses, this third one appears more plausible. From the point of view of international law, most cyber operations that have been either attributed or condemned by states may constitute, depending on the approach adopted, violation of territorial sovereignty. Among others, this was the case for the hack of Sony Picture Entertainment, the hack of the US Democratic National Party, NotPetya and WannaCry.21 The existence and content of the rule of sovereignty in cyberspace are not consensual among states. I am asserting that the existence of these diverging views and the observation that most cyber operations may constitute breaches of sovereignty may also explain, at least partly, the absence of international law in state practice.

States have expressed divergent views on sovereignty in cyberspace, even among the so-called ‘like-minded states’ There are three main unsettled issues.

First, the nature of ‘sovereignty’ is not settled. Sovereignty is a general principle of international law from which certain rules are derived, including prohibiting the violation of territorial sovereignty. In a nutshell, both rules and principles are sources of international law.22 Rules refer to the actual norms of international law, deriving notably from treaties or customary international law. Principles refer to the more abstract notions from which rules flow. While states agree on the existence of a general principle of sovereignty, they have divergent opinions on the rules flowing from that principle. Whereas some states and scholars consider sovereignty a principle of international law in the cyber realm, such as the United Kingdom, others argue that it is a rule, such as France, Iran and the Netherlands.23

Second, there is no consensus on what constitutes state sovereignty in cyberspace. For instance, there is debate over whether states are entitled to exercise sovereignty over data located on computers belonging to other entities which may or may not be inside the state’s territory. The confusion is amplified by conflation between sovereignty as a political concept and sovereignty from the international law perspective.

Third, there are multiple definitions of what constitutes a violation of territorial sovereignty when it comes to cyber operations. There are three main approaches. According to the first approach, any cyber operations penetrating a foreign system or producing effects over it constitute a violation of sovereignty. This is, for instance, the French approach (France 2019). The second approach claims that a cyber operation penetrating a foreign system constitutes a violation of sovereignty only if it meets a threshold of harm. This is the approach adopted by the Netherlands, the United States, and the Tallinn Manual 2.0 in its fourth rule.24 According to the third approach, territorial sovereignty cannot be breached by a cyber operation unless it constitutes a violation of the principle of non-intervention. This is, among others, the British approach (United Kingdom 2018b). Aside from these three approaches, the Tallinn Manual 2.0, and some states such as The Netherlands (2019), have laid out another basis that may constitute a breach of the rule of sovereignty: when ‘there has been an interference or usurpation of inherently governmental functions’.25 There are two criteria to sustain this one: first, it must concern ‘inherently governmental functions’; second, the concerned cyber operation should constitute an interference or usurpation of these functions.

Two comments can be inferred from this discussion on sovereignty. Firstly, the unsettled nature of sovereignty could have a chilling effect on the ability of attributing states to apply the concept in their attribution. The states usually involved in these attributions, such as Australia, The Netherlands, the United Kingdom and the United States, have different approaches on sovereignty and they may thus decide to avoid exposing their divergences in their coordinated attribution campaigns. Secondly, these condemned cyber operations may not constitute a violation of sovereignty for the attributing states, because their low intensity means that they may not cross the threshold of harm requested by some states.

This third hypothesis is most likely and could also explain why norms of responsible behaviour seem favoured in state practice. Indeed, even if the attributed or condemned behaviour would be lawful in certain cases, states may still contravene such norms. In such cases, the international legal responsibility of the sponsoring state cannot be invoked, but that state may be still held politically responsible. In addition, it should be noted that even if the condemned behaviour is lawful, international law allows the reacting state to adopt unilateral measures of retorsion against lawful but unfriendly acts (Delerue 2020, 424–433), such as certain sanctions or the expulsion of diplomats.

A fourth hypothesis could be that states want to avoid further development of international law, either by enshrining a specific interpretation of certain rules or principles in the cyber context, through the adoption of a new treaty or the development of customary international law.

On the interpretation, it must be recalled that a growing number of states are publicly expressing their approach to how the rules and principles of international law apply in cyberspace. Yet these statements—despite clarifying some very important issues of interpretation—remain relatively vague and abstract. On the contrary, in applying the legal reasoning to a specific case, states would have to go into greater details and nuances on these rules and principles in this specific case. There might be a fear, on the side of the attributing states, that such statements may be used by other states to condemn similar behaviours they would sponsor in the future and which could lead to the invocation of their legal responsibility. For instance, states are condemning cyber activities by taking advantage of the COVID-19 pandemic, such as espionage activities on the research to find a vaccine. It is to be expected that several states are conducting these espionage activities and the lack of reference to international law in their condemnation might be motivated by their fear to be similarly condemned and held legally responsible for their own activities (Delerue 2021). Similarly, on the treaty discussion, concrete case-study research on how international law has been applied in cyberspace could be instrumentalized to identify gaps and thus to justify the opening of a treaty-making process. By remaining abstract and vague on the application of international law to concrete cases, states limit this option.

The development of customary international law results from two cumulative elements: an opinio juris and a state practice. It is today possible to identify, to some extent, the opinio juris of states on these matters, for example through the work of the GGE and OEWG, the different declarations and statements, and publicly released documents on states’ approaches. On the other hand, the lack of concrete application of international law in their practice makes it more difficult, but not impossible, to study state practice. In that regard, for instance, the Non-Paper on Efforts to Implement Norms of Responsible State Behaviour in Cyberspace released in September 2019 by the UK Foreign and Commonwealth Office interestingly emphasized that ‘[w]hilst we continue to support and reinforce the application of international law in cyberspace, the activity set out in this paper is UK best practice and is not evidence of state practice with regard to the emergence of customary international law’ (United Kingdom 2019, 1).

Two hypotheses tested in this section seem plausible: on the one hand, most of the condemned behaviours are actually lawful and thus international law appears irrelevant (third hypothesis); on the other hand, states want to avoid further development of the international law applicable to cyber operations (fourth hypothesis).

Conclusion

This chapter highlights how international law seems to be rather absent in state practice and studied the reasons that may be behind this trend. From the four hypotheses tested, two appear plausible. First, states are not referring to nor applying international law in their public practice of attribution because most of the condemned behaviours are actually not internationally wrongful cyber acts. Thus, international law is irrelevant and the international legal responsibility of the sponsoring state cannot be invoked. For these reasons, norms of responsible behaviour appear to be a more accurate framework offering some political tools against such lawful behaviours. Second, states want to avoid the further elaboration of rules or principles of international law.
