6

Influence Operations in Cyberspace How They Really Work

Peter Pijpers and Paul Ducheine

HOW DO INFLUENCE OPERATIONS in cyberspace work? And what is the added value of cyberspace in that context? COVID-19 has been the latest, but will not be the last, pretext for spreading fabricated information. Polarizing topics like a global pandemic constitute an ideal opportunity for influencing foreign audiences in a deliberate way. While the spread of fabricated news is not a new phenomenon, cyberspace makes it faster and more effective. This chapter identifies two core tenets of why audiences are susceptible to influence operations in cyberspace: the exploitation of social media to magnify and amplify successful messages, and the ability to craft frames or scripts executed through disinformation campaigns. This chapter explores how these frames are designed by coupling a specific event, such as COVID-19, to a strategic narrative; refer to a divisive societal topic; and operate them by exploiting audiences’ preferences and heuristics. It concludes by arguing that the added value of cyberspace for spreading fabricated information resides in the ability to harvest data about the audience, generate audience’s heuristics, and reversely micro-target the audience with bespoke messages.

‘30 mila soldati dagli Usa in Europa senza mascherina’

‘Coronavirus? Macché, la Ue apre alle manovre Usa’

These headlines from the Italian news site of the daily Il Manifesto expressed surprise that while large parts of the Italy were in lockdown, the North Atlantic Treaty Organization was allowed to perform military exercises.1 Moreover, the articles critiqued NATO for apparently being exempt from the COVID-19 regulations, all of which had the seal of approval from the EU. COVID-19 is the latest, but likely not the last, pretext for framing news in a way that is beneficial to the one spreading it (Entman 1993, 52), in this case a left-wing outlet with a critical stance towards NATO. In general, COVID-19 proves to be a popular topic for spinning facts, populist outcries or fabricating fake news (Roberts 2020). Often these populist outcries are not picked up by the wider audience; they are solely consumed by the converted and will most likely not change most people’s perception, let alone their behaviour. But the spreading of framed or fabricated information can also come in a more methodical way as part of a deliberate disinformation campaign. Disinformation campaigns, as part of state-led influence operations, use similar platforms and events—including pandemics such as COVID-19, and earlier the downing of the Malaysian Airlines flight MH-17 or later the war in Ukraine—but the effect and intent of deliberate disinformation campaigns differ substantially from ad hoc fake news.2

Disinformation campaigns are not something new. Influencing audiences via disinformation campaigns has been ongoing for ages (Allan, Haiden and Reynolds 2018, 14–17), and especially during the Cold War–era both the United States and the Soviet Union, and their allies, were constantly spreading framed or misleading information, forgeries or propaganda (Kragh and Åsberg 2017). Disinformation, deception and masking the truth were elements of the US ‘political warfare’ (Robinson et al. 2018, 11 ff) and Soviet-Russian ‘active measures’ doctrines (Ajir and Vailliant 2018; Rid 2020). Often these disinformation campaigns, though a well-known political instrument, were unwelcome in the states that were being influenced. The rise of cyberspace, however, marks a new phase in this remit, one in which fabricated news can spread fast, reaching large audiences (Dragomir 2019, 158), and often these audiences are unaware of ongoing influence activities (Schmitt 2017, 320–321). Concern has risen about this digital manner of influence, especially related to foreign interferences regarding COVID-19 (Oxford Institute for Ethics Law 2020a). Initiatives to generate more transparency and predictable behaviour via norms in cyberspace have been called for (Internet Governance Forum 2018; Oxford Institute for Ethics Law 2020b; Global Commission on the Stability of Cyberspace 2019; UN General Assembly 2015).

Much has been written on disinformation operations, about influencing people and targeting groups via social media (Cohen and Bar’el 2017; Jeangène Vilmer 2018; Bayer et al. 2019). However, less is known on why people are even susceptible to certain influences via cyberspace and especially via social media. The core question to be answered in this chapter is therefore: How do influence operations generate effects via cyberspace? And what is the added value of cyberspace in that respect? Where appropriate, the influence operations will be illustrated by the 2020 disinformation campaigns related to COVID-19 in Italy, allegedly conducted by the Russian Federation (Digital Forensics Research Lab 2020a, 2020b; Sciubba Caniglia 2020).3 Following this opening section is a brief overview on what cyberspace entails and how we define influence operations for the purpose of this chapter. Next is an analysis of how influence operations could change the attitude and behaviour of target audiences via cyberspace, and how the use of cyberspace enhances influence operations. The final section provides some reflections on influence operations in cyberspace and whether current cyber-norm initiatives are adequate for regulating influence operations (see also Broeders 2021, 8–9).

The points of departure for the chapter are the following: First, the chapter focuses on state-level influence operations which remain below the threshold of the use of force.4 Second, influence operations use cyberspace as a vector to generate effects in the cognitive dimension; an influence operation does not affect cyberspace itself. And finally, fabricated news in this chapter is presented as part of a deliberately constructed informational element (e.g., dis-information) within a strategic plan of a state. Consequently, the often-used catch-all term ‘fake news’—which can include ‘white lies’, misinformation, manipulated political rhetoric and propaganda—will not be used.

Influence Operations in Cyberspace

Cyberspace as the ‘networked information infrastructure’ (Koh 2012, 6) is part of the information environment we live in.5 The information environment can be conceptually divided into three dimensions (Ducheine, van Haaster and van Harskamp 2017; Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff 2014) (figure 6.1). The physical dimension encompasses the globe and, furthermore, every conceivable tangible object (including people’s bodies). The cognitive dimension entails our individual and collective knowledge, perception, understanding, values and wisdom. The virtual dimension entails where and how information is collected, processed, stored, disseminated, and protected digitally. It is the digital or virtual reflection of the two other dimensions.

Cyberspace can be regarded as a part of the information environment, though the exact scope of cyberspace within the information environment depends on the perspective chosen. In this chapter, cyberspace entails those three layers that are unique to cyberspace, that is, the physical network layer—hence the hardware such as computers and glass fibre cables (Ducheine and Pijpers 2020, 24); the logical layer (i.e., the software and the data); and the virtual persona (such as user accounts) which are the reflections of (groups of) persons in the virtual dimension.

FIGURE 6.1
Information environment (Ducheine, van Haaster and van Harskamp 2017, 6; van Haaster 2018, 173 [n 898]).

The characteristics of cyberspace can be used to influence other actors distinct from the land or sea domain. Cyberspace is, in principle, boundless and ubiquitous, and there is a low threshold of admission which empowers non-state entities. Furthermore, in cyberspace the distinction between real and virtual disappears, in persons, acts or facts (Cohen and Bar’el 2017, 8–10). Finally, social media platforms allow for communication of all to all, enhancing behaviour based on the tendency to ‘like’ the posts of people who are similar or admired, rather than based on facts (see e.g., Bail et al. 2020). As elaborated below, the attributes of the virtual dimension of cyberspace are well-suited to deliberately manipulate the senses in order to alter the understanding of a situation and affect the subsequent autonomous decision-making process.

Influencing, related to the political realm, can be described as ‘the ability to persuade other(s) to do what one wants, or refrain from doing what one does not want’ (see e.g., Reich 2018, 179). More broadly, it is the activity to convince another actor that one’s views or opinions prevail over those of others, but also to prevent the view of the other actor from prevailing. Though influence operations have been described in numerous ways (Jeangène Vilmer 2018, 18; Cohen and Bar’el 2017, 7; Robinson et al. 2018, 1; Hollis 2018, 35–36, 41; Macdonald 2007, 32), common basic elements include the absence of the use of force or even warfare, the focus on the cognitive dimension and the objective of changing the behaviour of other actors directly or indirectly via a change in preferences and attitude. In this sense, behaviour is the physical manifestation of activities of an actor in its environment (Hutchinson 2010, 13–14), while attitude has a psychological and cognitive element related to values, culture, and beliefs of the psyche of an actor, society or political system, and is passive in nature (Fointiat and Barbier 2015, 2). An influence operation can be described as ‘the deployment of resources for cognitive ends that foster or change a targeted audience’s behaviour’ directly or via changing the attitude (Hollis 2018, 36). For this chapter, in which the use of force is excluded from the definition of influence, the core forms of influence are persuasion,6 coercion (or compellence) (Susser, Roessler and Nissenbaum 2019, 13–17), and manipulation, as depicted in figure 6.2. Persuasive influence operations aim to alter the autonomous decision-making of targeted audiences (state leadership, opinion leaders, mass public) (Hollis 2018, 35–36) by changing the weighing of the options to choose from, openly and willingly (Hollis 2018, 36; Lin and Kerr 2021). Persuasion entails an open appeal for a rational and conscious deliberation and subsequent choice (Susser, Roessler and Nissenbaum 2019, 14). The weighing can be changed by persuading the targeted audience to alter its understanding and perception of the environment, thereby converting the attitude7 of the audience (Cragin and Gerwehr 2005, 19). Altering the understanding, perception and worldview will subsequently influence the decision-making of the targeted audience in a way beneficial to the influencer (see also Lin and Kerr 2021), which in turn induces change in the behaviour of the targeted audience. During persuasive influence operations, the targeted audience still possesses, or perceives to possess, the ability to deliberatively understand and autonomously make decisions: the targeted audience makes a willing choice to change its behaviour (Hollis 2018, 36).8

Coercive influence operations can be explained in terms of an unwilling act (Wheatley 2019, 4), meaning that coercive operations do not provide meaningful options to the opposing audience, but limit or annihilate the decision-making options of a targeted audience.9 As there are no ‘acceptable alternatives’ (Wood 2014, 21), the opponent is forced to make an unwilling choice to act. Coercive operations, in contrast to persuasive influence operations, do not aim to change the attitude of targeted audiences (Borghard and Lonergan 2017, 453), but rather a change in behaviour (Fogg 2003, 8; Borghard and Lonergan 2017, 455; Schelling 1966, 3–4), aligning it with the demands of the coercer.10 Reversing this argument, an influence operation that shortcuts or circumvents deliberate understanding and autonomous decision-making is coercive in nature.

FIGURE 6.2
Understanding and decision-making.

A targeted audience or person that is subject to persuasive influence operations will keep the possibility of making a free choice, while coercive influence activities ‘robs them of choice’; in both cases, however, the ‘capacity for conscious decision-making remains intact’ (Susser, Roessler and Nissenbaum 2019, 15). This differs from manipulation, which attempts to subvert, undermine and even take control of the targeted audience’s capacity to make a decision. Manipulation does not aim to influence a belief, but uses psychological levers, heuristics and cognitive biases to achieve results, often in a subconscious, hidden or covert manner. Influence operations are deliberate activities targeting the cognitive dimension with the aim of changing the attitude or behaviour of the targeted audience. The dynamics of a conscious, overt persuasive and coercive influence operation might be obvious. But the question is how manipulative, subconscious, psychological and deceptive influence operations (via disinformation campaigns, etc.) generate effects and why audiences are susceptible to manipulative influence operations.

Generating Effects

Preparing the Influence Operation

Preparing a deliberate influence operation can be broken down by defining an intent and a strategic narrative, and operationalising the narrative; that is, by framing the narrative. Before engaging in an influence operation, the assertive state (the influencer) will define an intent. The intent serves its vital interests, is formulated in general political terms, and can change over time. The intent of the state is part of, or derives from, its worldview and perception of the world—based on collective cognition and knowledge of a society (history, collective memory, strategic culture, political and geospatial context). Related to the case in this chapter, the Russian Federation’s intent when executing influence operations in Western democratic states, in Italy (Antelava and Iacoboni 2020) among others, could best be described as creating strategic confusion,11 thereby alluding the success and strength of autocratic forms of government (Polyakova et al. 2016, 4). The Russian Federation also seeks to undermine democracy, sow distrust in Western institutions and destabilise societies, with the goal to control or ensure failure of European pro-Western government and leadership and weaken NATO and the EU (Radin, Demus and Marcinek 2020, 4–5).

If the assertive state decides to act or react based on its vital interests, its attitude is transformed into behaviour. The instruments of power, such as diplomacy, information, military, economy, culture and knowledge12 are the embodiment of the behaviour of the state and will be deployed to change the attitude and behaviour of the targeted state, with the aim to further and protect the national interests and values of the assertive state. Influence operations in cyberspace are manifestations of the informational instrument of power. Influence operations (below the threshold of the use of force) aim to alter or undermine the deliberate understanding and autonomous decision-making of targeted audiences (Ducheine and Pijpers 2020, 24–25), using a clear strategic and calibrated narrative as the main expression of power (Jeangène Vilmer 2018, 75). Though not unique to cyberspace, the strategic narrative is a deliberate, cognitive construct to unite groups of people and generate a shared view of a collective history and desired objective for the future (Dimitriu and De Graaf 2016, 6), examples of which are the concept of ‘American Exceptionalism’ (Lewis 2012, 108), Russkiy Mir (Bouwmeester 2020, 333–335), or the Israeli notion of the ‘Promised Land’. When used in an affirmative way, narratives aim to persuade or manipulate opponents by initiating the process of altering or counterbalancing their perceptions and beliefs and hence the context and weighing of the decision-making options. Related to the Russian intent to create strategic confusion in Western democracies, the generic narratives used relate to ‘anti-EU/Transatlantic’ sentiments (Antelava and Iacoboni 2020; Kragh and Åsberg 2017, 805), which are the corollary to the notion of Russkiy Mir.13 Compared to other European states, Italy has relatively close ties to the Russian Federation (Foy and Peel 2020). It stands to reason that the Russian disinformation campaign in Italy aims to strengthen the long-term popular sentiment towards Moscow and prevent a shift in favour of a dominant Euro-Atlantic position (Cristiani 2020).

Strategic narratives do not automatically influence a targeted audience. To operationalise a narrative, it will need to be broken down into simpler structures called frames or scripts (Lakoff 2009, 22 ff). Framing means to select and highlight some elements of a perceived reality and leave other elements out (Entman 1993, 52–55). The construction of the frame will depend on the manner of influence a state intends to pursue, ranging from persuasive influence activities to manipulative or coercive. Framing thus operationalises the strategic narrative and aims to create a script that will incline the audiences to make choices that are beneficial to the state executing the influence operation. Crafting a framed narrative therefore requires in-depth knowledge14 of key influencers and relevant societal topics but also ingrained preferences of the targeted audience.15 Hence, frames triangulate a) the strategic narrative, b) with divisive topics of a society and c) audiences’ preferences and heuristics. The trigger for exploiting a frame could be an event, such as an election or referendum, or a phenomenon such as the COVID-19 pandemic (figure 6.3).

FIGURE 6.3
Framing the narrative.

Socially divisive topics vary from state to state and depend on the attitude, perception and history of that state; there are inherently antagonistic dynamics within societies based on contrasting views, values and opinions of groups, and priors of the political leadership or the population at large. Moreover, shared practices, ideas and values are not fixed but in a continuous state of change (Moscovici 1998), such as the paradoxical trade-off between health, economy and the infringements on freedom in the age of COVID-19 (Georgieva and Ghebreyesus 2020). The essence of socially divisive topics, especially for democracies, is that these topics force groups in society to communicate (Nugent 2020, 11; Marková 2008, 42)16 in order to achieve or influence a common view and shared representation (Tansino 2019, 53), whether during election time, at ad hoc rallies or on late-night talk shows. Framing (in a manipulative manner) uses these natural seams caused by communication dynamics (Tansino 2019, 55) to address or inject topics that will raise discord in a society. The audiences’ cognitive and social heuristics or ‘rules of thumb’ (Kahneman 2003, 711) are psychological human reflexes. In general, targeted audiences are able to make deliberate choices following a rational decision-making process. However, in case of time constraints (Benson 2016), an overload of data (Lerman 2016, 8), or if the audience is not able to give meaning to that data or unable to align incoming information with existing patterns of thinking (Petty and Cacioppo 1986, 165–166), the rational manner of processing data is sidelined in favour of cognitive and social heuristics (Petty and Cacioppo 1986, 131–132).17

In general, these ‘mental shortcuts’ generate acceptable outcomes, but they can also lead to suboptimal judgments or biases. Feeding these shortcuts with fabricated content will add to biased judgment and blur the common understanding of a situation. Examples of cognitive heuristics are the biases related to focus (Korteling, Duistermaat and Toet 2018, 24–28), conformation, anchoring, stereotyping or oversimplifying, while social heuristics rely on authority, scarcity, reciprocity and social proof (Cialdini 2007). Russian ‘active measures’ rely on heuristics in what they call ‘reflexive control’ (Thomas 2004, 238–243), that is, ‘conveying to a partner or an opponent specially prepared information to incline him to voluntarily make the predetermined decision desired by the initiator of the action’ (Thomas 2004, 237; see also Ajir and Vailliant 2018, 72–73; Giles 2016, 19; Bouwmeester 2020, 38–56). With this mechanism, the Russian Federation seeks to influence the target audience in a subconscious manner utilising the cognitive biases which set in due to, among other factors, limitations in the available data and in human information processing capacity (Korteling, Brouwer and Toet 2018, 2).

Addressing socially divisive topics is still an overt and conscious way of persuading (or coercive when accompanied with a threat eliminating acceptable alternatives) an audience using arguments, logic and rationales (Wood 2014, 21–23). However, to generate a reflexive response to activities of influence operations and make audiences receptive to altering their behaviour, requires addressing the subconscious cognitive processes of groups and audiences (Susser, Roessler and Nissenbaum 2019, 16–17). The more influence operations rely on heuristics and covert subconscious techniques, the more manipulative the activity will be. Or as Lin argues, manipulation via influence operations is ‘the deliberate use of information by one party on an adversary population to confuse, mislead and ultimately influence the actions that the targeted population makes’ (Lin and Kerr 2021). Since cognitive and social heuristics cannot be addressed directly, a manipulative influence operation needs to adjust the form, context and content of a message (Korteling, Duistermaat and Toet 2018, 40–41) in order to trigger a reflexive response and sideline the rational decision-making process. The content, form and context of a message can be manipulated by what is presented (content), how it is it disseminated (the form or outlet of the content), and by tampering with the context in the information environment. Tampering with the context could include overloading the public sphere with too much data, withholding data, or limiting the time to assess the (one-sided) data. To facilitate the manipulative invocation of reflexive responses at the expense of a deliberate rational process, cyber-related activities can be executed using disinformation and trolling campaigns, the leaking of sensitive data,18 and political grooming.

Disinformation campaigns aim to sow discord among targeted audiences (Lanoszka 2019, 229; Digital Culture Media and Sport Committee 2018, 7–8; Mahairas and Dvilyanski 2018, 21), by deliberately spreading misleading, carefully constructed (Jeangène Vilmer 2018, 88), verifiably false (Allcott and Gentzkow 2017, 213), or falsified information. Misleading audiences can be effectuated by deceitful content, or by using a deceitful outlet or source that disseminates the content. During the 2016 US presidential elections, Russian agents mimicked US citizens while engaging in discussions on social media (US Senate Committee on Intelligence 2019, 30). The real US citizens (and voters) could therefore not value the authenticity of the content they received. Trolling (or mal-information campaigns) is the deliberate act of making discriminatory, abusive or otherwise controversial remarks on internet fora with the aim to harm persons, organisations or states (Wardle and Derakhshan 2017, 5; Sander 2019a, 377; US Senate Committee on Intelligence 2019, 18). The intent of trolling campaigns is to fuel extreme ideologies, hyperpartisan-ship, or conspiratorial political ideas purporting to polarise audiences (Martin and Shapiro 2019, 8).

Leaking of sensitive data is the revealing of non-public information into the public domain with the purpose of harming an individual, organisation or state (see also Kilovaty 2018, 149; Sander 2019b, 8–9). Leaked information is not necessarily false or manipulated. Leaking will sow doubt and confusion among the targeted audience because the information was not intended for public consumption, but also because the information will be disseminated from other outlets than expected.19 Political grooming (EU vs Disinformation 2019, 4) refers to an influence activity to discredit or favour a person of some authority (Jeangène Vilmer 2018, 77), whether incumbent or desiring to be. Political grooming revolves around the concept of infiltrating politics by cultivation of political allies (Polyakova et al. 2016, 3) and contains cyber-related techniques ranging from defamation, lobbying abroad (Ajir and Vailliant 2018, 80–81), foreign political advertising, party financing and supporting or manipulating diaspora or minorities abroad on political issues.

Executing Cyber-Related Activities

Influence operations can only be effective if the audience is susceptible to the framed narratives. Therefore, after operationalising the strategic narrative, the influence operation needs to be executed in or via cyberspace using activities in which the assertive state engages with (audiences of) the targeted state, whether the latter is aware of it or not. Based on the frames made, cyber-related activities are crafted and injected in the targeted state’s society, utilising the virtual dimension of cyberspace as a vector. Most often, the virtual persona layer (social media accounts) serves to sow confusion and subsequently tries to alter the perception of the targeted audiences by manipulating the content, form or outlet of a relayed message but also context, including by creating an information deluge (Lin and Kerr 2021). In March 2020, Italy was the first European state to implement a nationwide lockdown, becoming the frontline not only of concerns related to public health, but also of COVID-19 disinformation campaigns (Sciubba Caniglia 2020, 1–2). In three months, between 1 February and 30 April 2020, some 140 fabricated news articles related to Italy and COVID-19, stemming from Russian-sponsored news outlets, were published (EU vs Disinformation 2020a).20

The content that was shared was most often fabricated, false or at least misleading. Several strands of disinformation were used with the aim to drive a wedge between Italy and its European or Atlantic allies (Antelava and Iacoboni 2020) and undermine the credibility of the EU while strengthen the Russian–Italian ties. On 25 April 2020, Sputnik Italia announced that ‘Superior Friends’ would not allow European NATO and EU countries to request Russian aid (Sputnik Italia 2020a). A day earlier Sputnik quoted the Russian ambassador, arguing that while Italy was in the worst period of COVID-19, NATO and EU just stood by and watched (Sputnik Italia 2020b). These messages highlighted the suggestion that both organisations did nothing to support Italy in the dire times of COVID-19, while on the other side the Russian Federation was willing to help but not allowed to do so. Undermining stability in the EU and stressing the downsides of EU membership was highlighted in articles suggesting that Italy was better off leaving the EU. This strand of disinformation argued that due to COVID-19, the EU fell into a recession and weakened the solidarity between EU member states (One World 2020). It was also suggested that the COVID-19 virus was spread by the migrants that landed in Italy and—due to failing EU policies and lack of solidarity—had to stay there, which even led to riots in cities (Cristiani 2020, 3–4).

By using the topic of migrants spreading COVID-19, disinformation transgresses into trolling or mal-information operations with the aim to polarise groups within a society. The content used is meant for defamation rather than to mislead or to tweak reality. Trolling campaign were also used against Bill Gates,21 and even the disinformation campaign against NATO has elements of mal-information (NATO 2021). Where disinformation campaigns are a subtle expression of the frames used, trolling is a blunter or zero-sum instrument. The exaggerations made during trolling can therefore be viewed as ridiculous and obviously fake. However, to the converted it is a confirmation of existing beliefs, no matter how exaggerated the content is. On 14 May 2020 Sara Cunial, a member for the populist ‘Movimento 5 Stelle’, addressed the Italian Parliament to amplify COVID-19 disinformation and conspiracy rhetoric (Digital Forensics Research Lab 2020a),22 gaining notice by social media platforms tags such as ‘Noi stiamo con la Russia di Putin’23, ‘Italexit’, ‘Euroexit’ and ‘No5G Milano’. The content of the disinformation and trolling campaigns addressed topics which are socially divisive in Italy. They highlight the perceived decreasing ability of the EU in combating crises, but also underscore the lack of European solidarity, especially related to the reallocating migrants arriving from Northern Africa (Johnson, Fleming and Chazan 2020).

Russia used state-controlled outlets (e.g., Sputnik and RT) to disseminate the strands of disinformation and trolling campaigns. The deceptive element of these outlets is that they pretend to be, or present themselves as, genuine independent news outlet.24 Using both factual and fabricated data increases the confusion and exacerbates the misleading effect of the messages; the audiences were impaired in giving meaning to the information provided. Furthermore, their publications come in various languages, tailored to the targeted audiences (Kragh and Åsberg 2017, 781–782), in casu in Italian and not solely in Russian or English. The context can be tampered with by making use of an overload of information and leaking sensitive data. A deluge of political advertisements via social media would also contribute to hampering the value of the information (messages, Tweets or news articles) provided. Related to COVID-19, Italy witnessed a surge of messages spread and shared via social media, an infodemic that paralleled the COVID-19 pandemic (Massaro et al. 2021; Calamusa 2020). There is no evidence that Russian agents engaged on social media pretending to be Italian citizens, as was the case during the 2018 Italian elections (Martin and Shapiro 2019, 35). The Russian Federation did, however, support both Italian far-right and far-left parties (Polyakova et al. 2016, 4; Galeotti 2017, 2) in an attempt to scatter the political landscape and create confusion. For that same reason the Russian Federation also cultivates political extremes in other European states by (financially) supporting (or grooming) specific left-wing and right-wing anti-establishment or anti-EU parties in Europe (US Senate Committee on Foreign Relations 2018, 50 ff; Polyakova and Boyer 2018, 8; EU vs Disinformation 2019).

In the Italian case, disinformation and trolling campaigns were the cyber-related activities used most to articulate framed narratives in an attempt to invoke reflexive responses by manipulating the content, form and context of messages. The strands of the disinformation campaign were attuned to socially divisive topics including the effectiveness of EU policies (Johnson, Fleming and Chazan 2020). The strands were also adjusted to address sentiments within segments of the Italian society related to anti-NATO and anti-establishment inclination (EU vs Disinformation 2020b). The migration issue was linked to the COVID-19 pandemic and failing EU solidarity, and though the occurrences correlate, there is no causality between them.25 It can be deduced that the designed frames do not need to be true but must be framed as true and must ‘at least partially respond to reality, or at least accepted views’ (Ladislav Bittman, as quoted in Rid [2020, 4–5]).

Exploiting Social Media

After preparing an influence operation and executing the cyber-related activities such as disinformation campaigns, successful activities will need to be exploited. Similar to regular activities, whether military or marketing operations, after thorough preparation and execution, the output and the outcome must be assessed and evaluated after which new targets can be selected and exploited. The latter is even more relevant for influence activities in cyberspace, since these often depend on ambiguous, socially divisive topics and heuristics of the targeted audience and will therefore not have a predictable result26 based on causal relations (Rid 2020, 318–321).27 But the ones that do catch on need to be exploited.

Social media can be exploited to make the audience (even more) receptive to cyber-related activities such as disinformation campaigns by amplifying and magnifying messages, thereby creating the illusion of truth. In cyberspace, messages can be repeated almost indefinitely, making use of ‘bots’, a multitude of platforms, but also via ignorant social media users. Regarding the latter, communication in cyberspace is not dependent on specific outlets, government communiques or the high price of admission to broadcast. Everyone with an account and access to the internet can speak up and share opinions, views and emotions. In this context, Rid refers to outsourcing communication (2020, 434), since not only governmental agencies but everyone with a social media account (and connection) is a communicator.

Apart from the human activity in creating and repeating messages, as with the Italian case where the Russian social media platform VKontakte picked up the anti-NATO narrative related to the COVID-19 crisis in Italy (Digital Forensics Research Lab 2020b), disinformation can be repeated and reinforced via software programmes, the so-called bots (Jeangène Vilmer 2018, 85; Macdonald 2007, 83 ff; Howard, Woolley and Calo 2018, 82–84). Bots are used to generate support for politicians by amplifying and disseminating media messages on platforms including Twitter, Facebook or Instagram, but can also exploiting social media by repeating the message on a multitude of platforms. The amplification of messages creates an illusion of proof and authority. If many agree, support or ‘like’ a person or message, humans—as social beings—are inclined to perceive this as being familiar, true, genuine or authoritative, addressing heuristics of the audiences.28 This illusionary connection, and addictive inclination, related to the number of likes instead of the facts, can be used as an instrument of exploitation (Singer and Brooking 2018, 3; Pomerantsev 2019, 161). Or as DeMarzo, Vayanos and Zwiebel (2003, 911–912) argue, repetition of statements increases the subject’s belief in their validity, making them more familiar, and familiarity serves as a cue to validity (DeMarzo, Vayanos and Zwiebel 2003, 911–912).

The Added Value of Cyberspace

This chapter focused on how influence operations generate effects via cyberspace and what the added value of cyberspace in this context is. The cognitive dimension of the targeted audiences can be affected or influenced in three ways: persuasion, coercion or manipulation. Where persuasion and coercion rely on overt and rational techniques, manipulative influencing tries to use hidden and subconscious manners. The aim of manipulative influence operations is to invoke the heuristic (peripheral) manner of processing information, sidelining the deliberate understanding and autonomous decision-making and luring targeted audiences into making biased judgments. Target audiences can be deflected to heuristic and biased judgments when obtaining too much data, having too little time to process it, or not being able to attach significance to the data or see when an internal conflict arises between new, incoming data versus existing, embedded data. To create these circumstances, influence operations will manipulate the content of a message, the outlet (or form) of the message and/or the context by meddling with the amount of data provided to the targeted audience. Related to the Italian case, cyber-related activities that appeal to these circumstances are primarily disinformation and trolling campaigns.

Disinformation or trolling campaigns are not new as instruments of influence or ‘political warfare’ (see Holden 1988, 665 related to alleged dis-information on AIDS; Cull et al. 2017, 1–6). What is new is the context and the possibilities of cyberspace. In the first place, the attributes of cyberspace and especially social media platforms make it possible to micro-target very specific groups in society because of the data regarding the behaviour and attitude of these groups that is available on social media.29 Moreover, social media offers opportunities to extract socially divisive topics from (foreign) societies and the ingrained preferences of specific groups within that society. This data, related to what triggers societies to start communicating and what reflexive responses are likely to follow, is required to operationalise (frame) the strategic narrative of the influencing state. Frames highlight specific elements of the perceived reality, thereby coupling the strategic narrative to divisive topics within a society and audiences’ preferences and heuristics around a specific event such as COVID-19. As a result, these audiences can receive bespoke content, in a language that they comprehend, and via a medium that they use (Information Commissioner’s Office 2018a, 22 ff). These micro-targeting techniques can have a positive effect in mobilising groups to engage in political or societal discourse, but can also be used to generate disengagement and polarisation. The frames made do not need to be true but must perceived to be so.

Apart from crafting tailor-made frames and subsequent disinformation campaigns, the attributes of cyberspace offer the ability to repeat and magnify the strands of cyber-related activities including disinformation and trolling campaigns. Influence operations can exploit social media to magnify and amplify those cyber-related activities to which the audience is initially receptive. In cyberspace, bots and algorithms can be used to repeat content, and by using not one but multiple social media platforms or news outlets, the targeted audience is overwhelmed with (often) one-sided information. Moreover, due to the speed and reach of the internet, the exploitation of successful cyber-related activities runs in parallel with the cyber-related activity itself, reinforcing its effect.

Tailor-made framing and the exploitation of social media are illustrative for influence operations that make use of cyberspace. Due to the attributes of cyberspace, traditional operations differ significantly from cyber-related influence operations. In general, influence operations follow a sequence of preparing, executing, and exploiting the cyber-related influence activities. Though the phases can be distinguished in theory, these are not chronological phases in practice. Not only is it possible for the executing and exploitation phase to run in parallel, but it is also conceivable that states execute only some of the phases of the influence operations. Moreover, it cannot be taken for granted that influence operations are prepared, executed, and exploited by one state. A state can choose to employ a full influence operation in which preparation, execution and exploitation are state-led; or it can choose a piecemeal approach where the state engages during phases or subphases supporting and enhancing existing domestic influence operations, as was the case in the Italian example examined here. It stands to reason that Italy was not the main target of the Russian influence operations, but rather instrumental to the Russian Federation’s anti-EU narrative. A vast number of (fabricated or misleading) articles from Russian media outlets related to EU support to Italy during COVID-19 were written in the Slavic languages, hence meant for local or domestic audiences. The COVID-19 crisis was merely used as a pretext to start the influence operation (van der Made 2020). Framing, in this case, was partially domestic, but the enhancement and exploitation was driven by the Russian Federation as the Italian disinformation campaign coalesced with existing Russian anti-EU and anti-NATO narratives.30

The increased intrusiveness of influence operations making use of cyberspace would arguably justify deliberations on additional norms for state behaviour in cyberspace. Apart from the discourse on how to apply international law to cyberspace in situations below the threshold of the use of force, norms can support transparency and predictability (United Nations General Assembly 2021, 4). And though the UN GGE and OEWG processes slowly gain traction, the lion’s share of cyber norms that are developed relate to the physical network layer of cyberspace (Hollis and Neutze 2021, 381, n. 14), and not to influence operations that use cyberspace as a vector.31 Formulating cyber norms for cross-border influence operations will be challenging since the crystallisation of norms requires commensurate state behaviour (Schmitt 2020, 36). And the opportunities to opt in or out and to align foreign to domestic influence operations make it difficult to assess, or even to speak about, a coherent or traditional Russian influence operation, let alone to apply or develop norms of accepted behaviour.

Notes

1. The news articles were published in March 2020 via https://ilmanifesto.it/. A liberal translation of the headlines is: ‘Thirty thousand US soldiers are in Europe without protective face masks’; ‘Coronavirus, not at all! The EU is open to US manoeuvres’ (see Dinucci 2020a; 2020b).

2. See also Baade (2018), who differentiates between distorted and false news.

3. Sciubba Caniglia argues that also China and the US executed (dis)information activities in Italy.

4. That is below the use-of-force threshold as found in Article 2(4) of the UN Charter or in the jus cogens rule on the prohibition on the threat or use of force; see Stephens (2020, 4–5). Of note, this restriction does not exclude the possibility that influence operations can and will be used during armed conflicts or war as a supporting or supported line of operations to achieve a goal.

5. Or as Kuehl (2009, 28) describes it, cyberspace is ‘global domain within the information environment whose distinctive and unique character is framed by the use of electronics and the electromagnetic spectrum to create, store, modify, exchange, and exploit information via interdependent and interconnected networks using information communication technologies’.

6. Fogg (2003, 8) defines persuasion as ‘an attempt to change attitudes or behaviors or both (without using coercion or deception)’. See also Petty and Briñol (2008, 137) and Fointiat and Barbier (2015, 2).

7. The most common target of persuasion is a person’s attitude, being the primary object of influence due to the presumed influence on choices and action; see Petty and Briñol (2008, 137).

8. A persuasive influence operation is aligned with Hollis’s (2018) influence operations, which exclude coercion.

9. Or as Appelbaum (2015, 112) states, coercion is ‘universally recognized as vitiating that person’s decision and thus rendering consent invalid’.

10. Compliance can be achieved by coercion and enticement, and is a short-term change in behaviour (Cragin and Gerwehr 2005, 15–20).

11. Also referred to as plausible deniability, or maintaining an air of plausibility; see Kragh and Åsberg (2017, 778, 789); see also Broad (2020) and Jeangène Vilmer (2018, 75).

12. Without being exclusive. The instruments of power are often archetypically abbreviated as DIME, but can also include infrastructure, legal or financial instruments.

13. An unstable European Union and a weak Euro-Atlantic relation would ipso facto generate the perception of a strong and stable Russian Federation.

14. Including demographic data of the target audience, such as age, gender, education, savings, location. See Information Commissioner’s Office (2018a, 22).

15. It can be argued that influence operations during the 2017 French elections (allegedly by Russian and American alt-right activists) were not successful, partly due to lack of knowledge of French language and culture. See also Jeangène Vilmer (2019) and Ribeiro et al. (2019, 140–149).

16. Or paraphrasing Moscovici, ‘there would be hardly any reason to communicate if there were no tensions, asymmetries or conflicts between interacting parties’ (in Tansino [2019, 54]).

17. In their ‘elaboration likelihood model’, Petty and Cacioppo (1986) refer to the central versus the peripheral route. The central route would require substantial elaboration (taking into account issue-relevant argument) while the peripheral route relies on heuristic processes. To Petty and Cacioppo this is not a dichotomy but a continuum.

18. Such as during the 2016 US and the 2017 French presidential campaigns (see Mueller 2019; Jeangène Vilmer 2019).

19. Leaking or whitewashing operations are often preceded by a computer intrusion–a hack. See also Mueller (2019, 36 ff).

20. Though not all were directed at Italian audiences. Numerous articles highlighted the EU imperfections for domestic or regional audiences in, for example, Armenia (see Sputnik Armenia 2020).

21. Arguing that COVID-19 is a US-borne virus financed by Bill Gates, implanting microchips to control people in order to create a global surveillance network or to decrease the world population (see Digital Forensics Research Lab 2020a).

22. Of note, MP Sara Cunial was elected to represent the Five Star Movement (M5S) but expelled due to her extreme stances.

23. Meaning ‘we stand with Putin’s Russia’ (see also https://www.facebook.com/1761482714098169/posts/2641809226065509).

24. Though already in 2017 the French president had accused Sputnik and RT of being Russian ‘agents of influence’ (see Louis 2017).

25. The 2016 UK Referendum on the EU also saw frames in which long-standing resentment against the EU were linked to the economic malaise and migrant issues. Another example is the so-called German ‘Lisa case’ (see also Ball 2017, 60; Mahairas and Dvilyanski 2018, 21–27).

26. After the explosion of the Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant in 1986, the Soviet KGB forged letters from the US Intelligence Services and leaked them to US newspapers. The latter did not pick up the story.

27. The concept that a change in attitude results in a change in behaviour is not uncontested. Ajzen and Fishbein (1980) argue that there is a causal sequence going from belief to attitude, social norm, intention and behaviour. Others, like Fointiat and Barbier (2015, 1) do not believe a clear causal relation exists but urge for more research on this topic.

28. What Cialdini calls ‘social proof’ (2007, 114 ff).

29. Knowledge of the preferences or biases of the audiences and target groups must be obtained based on political preferences, ethnic background, voting behaviour, online consumer and social behaviour, mode of transport and so on. The data can be harvested from platforms like Facebook. See also Information Commissioner’s Office (2018b, 16–18.)

30. The director of the Rome-based International Institute of Strategic Studies stated that ‘Moscow is using the Covid-19 outbreak “to strengthen anti-EU feelings and to reinforce the impression that the EU is crumbling, to make propaganda gains and gather intelligence at the heart of NATO”’ (in Antelava and Iacoboni 2020).

31. See Schmitt (2017), Rule 4 on violations of sovereignty and Rule 66 on the prohibition of intervention.
