10

Will India Negotiate? The Politics of Multilateral Engagement for Fostering Responsible State Behaviour in Cyberspace

Arindrajit Basu and Karthik Nachiappan

MULTILATERAL AND MULTISTAKEHOLDER initiatives attempting to foster responsible state behaviour in cyberspace continue to remain fragmented. With a large and diverse set of users coming online, India deeply matters to these global cyber discussions. Despite being a member of five out of the six UN Group of Government Experts (GGE) processes to date, India is yet to publicly articulate its position on the outcomes or workings of these processes, or to make public their submissions on the Open-Ended Working Group (OEWG) process. This reticence exists in stark contrast to India’s positions on the question of cross border data flows at digital trade discussions, where India has been vocal on various several issues and a flagbearer for ‘data sovereignty.’ Research indicates that, historically, India’s approach toward multilateral negotiations have been hybrid, resting on whether and how rules being negotiated advance economic or security interests, the incumbent capacity to engage with multilateral negotiation, and domestic interests at play (Nachiappan 2019). Where interests were clear and institutional capacity robust, Indian officials enthusiastically negotiated and signed international rules, but these two conditions rest on how systemic developments affect India’s economic trajectory and security outlook. These trends have existed across multilateral negotiations on tobacco control, trade, nuclear weapons , and climate change (Nachiappan 2019). This chapter makes a new contribution to existing literature by assessing these factors with respect to cyberspace and comparing them with India’s participation in negotiation processes across regimes previously studied. In doing so, it attempts to understand how India might engage with fragmented cyber norms processes in the near future.1

This chapter limits itself to studying India’s participation in “cyber norms fora”—multilateral fora seeking to devise norms of responsible behaviour in cyberspace for states—the most prominent of which are at the United Nations First Committee, but also pivot through other private-sector and multistake-holder initiatives (Ruhl et al. 2020). While we refer to other digital regimes in cyberspace, we do not study in detail global fora on internet governance that look to define the governance of the internet architecture, or global debates on digital economy that govern trade, e-commerce and cross-border data flows. While these three regimes often converge and are linked, for the purpose of analytical clarity we retain this distinction throughout our chapter.

The chapter is divided into three sections. First, we unpack the factors driving India’s multilateral behaviour. Second, we unpack India’s participation at cyber norms fora and explain this by referring to the analytical framework described in the first section. Finally, we identify factors that might shape the extent and nature of India’s engagement with cyber norms processes in the immediate future—until the culmination of the present UN GGE and OEWG processes. We do not recommend strategies for negotiation or offer recommendations on positions that should be adopted by India, but limit this chapter to outlining the factors that have driven India’s stance thus far, and those that may continue to drive it.

Factors Driving India’s Multilateral Behaviour

India has often been regarded as an obstructionist at multilateral fora, blocking multilateral rules or failing to make a concrete impact at multilateral negotiations (Malone 2011; Mohan 2010). Scholars have chronicled how and where India has fallen short when it comes to addressing salient global governance challenges. Adjectives used to describe India’s multilateral postures and contributions include ‘defensive’, ‘prickly’ and ‘obstructionist’. Reasons given to explain this ostensible defensive proclivity have ranged from specific cultural traits (Narlikar 2006, 2013, 2014, 2017), institutional preoccupations of the Ministry of External Affairs (MEA) that tilt toward reticence and defensiveness (Malone 2011; Cohen 2001; Schaffer and Schaffer 2016), ideological posturing (Sullivan 2014; Miller and Sullivan de Estrada 2017), and various political strategies employed by Indian negotiators that constrain India from making meaningful concessions (Narlikar 2006; Hurrell and Narlikar 2006). Unavoidably, the politics of the Global South has coloured India’s approach on specific multilateral issues and scholarship, particularly on issues like trade, arms control and climate change, has amply outlined that perspective (Prashad 2013; Hurrell and Narlikar 2006; Dubash 2013). Such interpretations depict an India that relentlessly defies international edicts; to put it concretely, specific notions of identity, victimhood, material constraint and status as a developing country apparently trigger an adversarial India when negotiating multilateral issues.

Such explanations, however, conceal as much as they reveal; disconcertingly, their pervasive grip absolves us from soberly measuring whether political economy factors, shaped by India’s growing economic and security imprint, influence India’s multilateral positions. Rising interdependence will leave India little choice but to engage and participate on multilateral issues, like cybersecurity, that affect its growth and security. Burgeoning interdependence shifts the political calculus of actors, both state and non-state, who would ostensibly seek to either accelerate or hamper India’s engagement with the international order. As India further embeds itself within the international order and engages on issues such as internet governance and cybersecurity, it is important to robustly track and show how and whether this integration shapes how India negotiates international rules.

While rhetoric on defending the interests of developing nations as a collective endures, recent research has shown that the nature and scope of India’s engagement come down to three factors: national interests, institutional capacity to negotiate the issue, and how domestic interest groups influence institutional views before and during negotiations. In Does India Negotiate?, Nachiappan (2019) proves this thesis by an empirical assessment of India’s participation in four multilateral negotiations: the Framework Convention on Tobacco Control (FCTC), 2003, the Comprehensive Test Ban Treaty (CTBT), 1996, the Uruguay Round that set up the World Trade Organisation (WTO), 1993, and the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), 1992. The findings in the book also apply to India’s participation in anti-terror negotiations, which we use as supporting evidence.

National Interests (Economic and Security)

Interests determine India’s multilateral stances (Nachiappan 2019, 191). On tobacco control, Indian health officials were aligned with the World Health Organisation (WHO) towards setting up a robust convention that created clear rules regulating tobacco production and distribution globally. There was a strong domestic recognition of the societal and health impacts of tobacco use, which prompted India to enact domestic legislation constraining the same and thereby taking a keen stance on several provisions of the Framework Convention on Tobacco Control (FCTC) ‘that had many similar provisions to India’s tobacco control bill’ (WHO n.d.). Despite being opposed to the position charted out by the United States, at the behest of the American lobby, the FCTC came into force.

India’s preference for a strong Comprehensive Test Ban Treaty (CTBT) in 1993 was a result of several years of diplomacy seeking to reduce the gap between nuclear weapon states and India (Nachiappan 2019, 192). The Indian delegation maintained its stance on the need for a treaty that banned all kinds of nuclear tests, peaceful or otherwise (‘Conference on Disarmament’ 1996). Arundhati Ghose, India’s chief negotiator, stated that ‘the political intent needs to be reflected in the CTBT, clearly defining the objective to bring to an end all nuclear weapons development’ (‘India urges linking’ 1996). Ghose (1996) underscored these concerns by calling out countries that ‘continue their nuclear weapons programs in a complex or in a clandestine way’.

While strategic interests generally shape any country’s multilateral engagement, this statement matters significantly for India. It is important to note that the MEA budget and personnel are smaller than that of other countries (Marlow 2018). This scenario means that, notwithstanding the competence of Indian diplomats, there are constraints on the number of issues India can robustly engage with, and strategic interests and priorities clearly augment or constrain the extent of engagement with a particular issue (Madan 2013, 97).

Institutional Views and Capacity (Government)

Domestic institutions also matter, or how policy-makers confront an issue in India and the institutional capacity across government entities to adequately engage with that problem. That said, the capacity to negotiate and ratify multilateral rules does not solely remain within the MEA, but extends across ministries. Instead, the Indian bureaucracy’s approach to an issue, and the views and capacity of the specific institution tasked with shepherding negotiations, must be considered in evaluating this prong. India’s position at the 2003 FCTC negotiations was shaped by robust institutional views on tobacco control. Similarly, before the Uruguay Round negotiations, Indian officials apprised themselves of the constraints Indian businesses faced while exporting textiles, agricultural goods and services abroad. Armed with the knowledge of the contours of the domestic market, Indian negotiators were able to extract the optimal level of protection for the Indian market while trying to obtain greater market access for Indian goods and services. The Ministry of Commerce was the key institution during these negotiations, which was guided by input from experts at the Indian Institute of Foreign Trade (IIFT).2 The Ministry of Commerce commissioned a report by the IIFT, goading the negotiators to remain cautious on trade in services while emphasizing labour services at the negotiations (McDowell 1997, 126).

At the CTBT negotiations, MEA officials were able to leverage decades of experience, posturing for a nuclear test ban treaty and coordinating with other agencies working on India’s nuclear program, such as the Indian Atomic Energy Association (Malik 2016, 197). Indian diplomats leveraged think-tanks in New Delhi, such as the Centre for Policy Research, and organised a seminar in December 1995 to evaluate India’s approach at the CTBT negotiations that brought strategic experts and retired diplomats into the conversation (Nachiappan 2019, 121). India’s approach at the UNFCCC was driven by the MEA rather than the Ministry of Environment and Forests (MOEF). This resulted in India’s approach being more ‘political than environmental in tenor’ and was propelled by a resistance to constraints that would halt India’s development trajectory (Dasgupta 2011, 217).

Domestic Stakeholders (Non-Governmental) and Political Interests

Finally, as with any other country, India’s negotiating stances and enthusiasm has been guided by the preferences of several non-governmental stake-holders and domestic political realities. To be sure, not all interest groups have equal access or influence in shaping India’s approach at negotiations. Experts, civil society groups, and business lobbies have all shaped India’s approach. At UNFCCC (1992) negotiations, The Energy and Resources Institute (TERI) served as a key resource.3 While its initial goal (when it was established in 1974) was to champion public discourse on limiting pollution and resulting environmental harm, it grew by 1986 to ‘filling gaps in governmental services and knowledge related to energy matters’ (Jakobsen 1998, 16). Another institution that became involved in the negotiations was the Centre for Science and Environment (CSE), which stressed equity. In a report titled Global Warming in an Unequal World (Agarwal and Narain 1991), the heads of the CSE criticised a World Resources Institute report released just before the UNFCCC negotiations in 1991 which argued that ‘developing countries [Brazil, India, China] were just as responsible’ for climate change as the developed world (World Resources Institute 1990). CSE’s report argued that the World Resources Institute report was based ‘less on science and more on politically motivated jugglery; (Agarwal and Narain 1991) and served as a game-changer in the political discourse on climate change. While there were institutional blind spots—scientific institutions working on climatology and meteorology rarely interacted with policy institutions like TERI—the insights provided by TERI and CSE were instrumental in advising Indian officials on the political and policy risks of reducing emissions budgets and forming a stance emphasizing equity, eschewing joint action and responsibility by both the developed and developing world.4 During the Uruguay Round negotiations, Indian business groups—the Confederation of Engineering Industry, Federation of Indian Chambers of Commerce, Associated Chamber of Commerce and the National Association of Software and Service Companies—influenced the Indian delegation’s views on intellectual property rights and trade in services as part of the negotiations.

India and the Global Debate on Cyber Norms

Has India Negotiated?

India has been recognized as a key ‘digital decider’ (Arampatzis 2018) of the future of the internet, and the values that govern it. Yet, on cyber norms formulation specifically, India has largely remained noncommittal. The clearest articulation of India’s stance on international law in cyberspace came from then Deputy National Security Adviser Arvind Gupta during a speech at the Institute of Defense Studies and Analysis where he stated: ‘In India’s point of view, Tallinn Manual, while being a useful exercise, does not reflect the existing law on the subject because of the absence of state practice which is critical for development of customary international law’ (Gupta 2016). However, in the four years since that speech, India has yet to articulate a statement on how international law applies in cyberspace or anything remotely as clear as Dr. Gupta’s denouncement of the Tallinn Manual. Several other countries including the United Kingdom, France, Germany, Estonia, Cuba, China, Iran, the Netherlands, and the United States5 all made their positions clear in a bid to shape the ongoing debate. Indian delegations have made some broad statements at the 70th and 71st sessions6 of the UN General Assembly but have not put forth any substantial additions to the existing debate. India’s interventions at the OEWG are largely reaffirmations of points already raised7 and debated by other states8 or limited to language edits on drafts of the OEWG report (‘India’s remarks/comments’ 2021). The two positions that India has consistently advocated for during publicly available OEWG conversations revolve around trusted supply chains and the protection of critical information infrastructure.9 India also raised the need for a norm condemning offensive cyber operations at the First Substantive Session of the OEWG, and endorsed such a norm along with Indonesia, Iran, Pakistan, Cuba and Nigeria (Gavrilovic 2019). However, the need for this norm, or its absence in the final report, found no mention either in the ‘Explanation of Position’ or comments on the Zero Draft submitted by the Indian delegation towards the end of the process in 2021.

India’s voting patterns and participation in coalitions only confound the uncertainty and lack of clarity. In November 2018, it voted for both the US-backed resolution which set up the sixth UN GGE, and the Russian-backed resolution that set up the UN OEWG. Similarly, India has also raised eyebrows by voting for a controversial Russian-backed resolution looking to set up an international convention against cybercrime at the United Nations Third Committee. This resolution and the subsequent treaty have been criticised for laying a launch pad for government censorship. The voting patterns at the Third Committee were similar to the fissures brewing in the First Committee on cyber norms, and India’s voting at the Third Committee was interpreted by some as a possible alignment with the Sino-Russian version of the internet (Sherman 2019).

Yet, earlier this year, India entered two limited democratic coalitions—the ‘D10 on 5G’ and the Global Partnership on Artificial Intelligence—comprising largely of Group of 7 (G7) countries with the notable exclusion of China and Russia (Basu and Sherman 2020). The D10 coalition on 5G technologies comprises Group of 7 members along with Australia, India and South Korea and was set up with the explicit aim of devising alternatives to Chinese supply of 5G technologies. The Global Partnership on AI was created with a focus on developing global rules of the road for the governance of AI technologies, and includes G7 countries along with India, the Republic of Korea, Singapore, Slovenia and the European Union. Both groups notably exclude China and Russia and were clearly created to align with the US and Western bloc’s positions at the UN debates. In this context, India’s refusal to sign the Paris Call for Trust and Security in Cyberspace, championed by Microsoft and the French government, requires further examination (‘Davos leaders urge India’ 2019). Some sources claim that the reason for this may have been a reference to the Budapest Convention in the text of the Paris Call, which India is not a signatory to, but no public justification has been provided by New Delhi yet.10

This confusing, ambiguous and passive stance can be explained by considering the formula we outlined in the previous section. Despite clear economic and security interests in cyberspace, India does not view shaping the norms formulation debate as a means of furthering these interests. In line with a declared realist shift in Indian foreign policy with the present National Democratic Alliance government (Jaishankar 2020), India remains ambiguous, allowing it to use ongoing negotiations to further short-term interests without accepting long-term commitments. Existing institutions working on cyber diplomacy are aplenty, but there is a lack of apparent coordination between the various branches, further contributing to the lack of a stance that accommodates the expertise and perspective of all these institutions.11 Furthermore, despite a vibrant civil society, industry heavily dependent on digital India, and a political economy organized around protecting the same, none of these stakeholders have chosen to lobby the government on the cyber norms debate—choosing instead to focus on other issues in the digital economy. Therefore, the impetus to negotiate on behalf of a certain domestic constituency or to further domestic political interests has also been missing. Below, we explain how each of the factors that have shaped vibrant participation at other multilateral fora have failed to do so in the cyber norms debate.

National Interests (Economic and Security)

India’s strategic interests in cyberspace are clear as its threats in cyberspace largely emanate from ongoing (kinetic) conflict with neighbours. China and China-linked actors have stepped up the use of cyber operations on critical infrastructure in India, especially after border tensions broke out between India and China in 2020 (Sanger and Schmall 2021). There is no public evidence yet of India deploying cyber operations against China, despite calls from security experts to do so (Singh 2020).12

In recent years, both India and Pakistan have stepped up their cyber warfare capabilities and engaged in low-intensity cyberattacks, not amounting to the use of force as per Article 2(4) of the United Nations Charter (Qadeer 2020). Unlike the Sino-Indian case, where a majority of attacks have been perpetrated by China, India-Pakistan relations are characterised by tit-for-tat attacks, through which both sides orchestrate non-state actors to engage in low-scale attacks in response to cyber or physical aggression from the other.13 Indian government websites have been targeted by Pakistani non-state groups through defacement efforts. Both the Indian and Pakistani Advanced Persistent Threats (APTs) continue to engage in national security espionage against each other (Baezner 2018, 8).

Given these interests, it is clear that India would benefit from being able to shape the rules of the road in a manner that constrains attacks from adversaries, while enabling India to chart out its own offensive and defensive strategies in cyberspace. Instead, India has opted for ad hoc strategic moves that yield short-term dividends. Unlike the CTBT negotiations, in which India had been entrenched for some time, the nascency of global cyber negotiations means that India has yet to devise a method through which these short-term interests can frame a long-term multilateral strategy. For example, India has banned a number of Chinese apps in the aftermath of clashes on the Sino-Indian border. As per a press release put out by the government, ‘These apps are engaged in activities which [are] prejudicial to sovereignty and integrity of India, defence of India, security of state and public order’ (Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology 2020). While this move furthered Indian strategic interest in restricting Chinese inroads into India’s digital economy, no explanation was provided on how these apps threaten India’s sovereignty or security. Nor was there an effort made to convince the international community of the validity of these restrictions through reference to international or domestic law, or norms for responsible behaviour.

Institutional Views and Capacity (Government)

The lack of clear engagement does not stem from a lack of understanding of strategic interests alone. Indian cyber diplomacy efforts have historically been plagued by an uncoordinated and undefined institutional setup, where institutions are unclear on their own scope and responsibilities, and therefore have not coordinated with other institutions to clearly chart out domestic or global stances (Ebert, Saslow and Wezling 2020). The lack of coordination has been responsible for an incoherent approach to stances adopted at internet governance fora (Abraham et al. 2018). But this scenario has changed over the past year with the setting up of institutions acknowledging the need for a more coordinated approach to cybersecurity and a clearly articulated cyber doctrine (Chawla 2020b).

There are three ministries that shape India’s institutional views and engagement on norms of responsible state behaviour: the Ministry of Defense (MoD), the MEA and the Prime Minister’s Office (PMO) (Ebert, Saslow and Wezling 2020, 10). The MoD manages India’s cyber defence with three agencies under its administrative control. First, the Defence Intelligence Agency (DIA) analyzes and coordinates military intelligence gathered from the three other branches of the armed forces.14 The second entity under its control is the Defense Cyber Agency (DCyA), a tri-service command of the Indian Armed Forces which draws about 1,000 personnel from all three branches of the armed forces. The existing Defence Information Assurance and Research Agency (DIARA) was upgraded to form DCyA. As announced by the prime minister at the Combined Commanders Conference in 2018, both DIARA and its predecessor Defense Information Warfare Agency came under the aegis of DIA, along with the similar agencies for space and special operations (‘PM Narendra Modi attends’ 2018). DCyA is governed by the Integrated Defence Staff (Ebert, Saslow and Wezling 2020, 10). The third and final body is the Defence Research and Development Organisation, the leading agency for research and development evaluation engaged in developing a variety of defence technologies.15 While the Ministry of Defense has not—and will not—represent India in global negotiations, its agencies are responsible for articulating offensive and defensive cyber postures, which should shape the stances India adopts at the UN GGE and OEWG, and also determine the multilateral or multistakeholder coalitions India chooses to join.

The PMO has several agencies and individuals with a cyber portfolio (Ebert, Saslow and Wezling 2020, 11). The National Security Council, chaired by the National Security Advisor, includes the prime minister and several cabinet ministers and oversees national security, intelligence and other issues of strategic concern, including cybersecurity. The National Security Council’s Joint Intelligence Committee analyses information aggregated by India’s intelligence agencies but does not include cyber operations within its remit. The PMO also created the position of the National Cyber Security Coordinator (NCSC) in March 2015, which is part of the National Security Council Secretariat and advises the NSA and PMO on cybersecurity matters (Ebert, Saslow and Wezling 2020, 11). The National Technical Research Organisation is a technical intelligence agency reporting to the National Security Advisor in the PMO, set up in 2004.16 Reports emerged in 2012 stating that the DIA and the National Technical Research Organisation were tasked with carrying out offensive cyber operations, if necessary (Sridhar 2012). The PMO does not take the lead on representing India at global fora, but their input as a nodal agency and functioning to co-ordinate the various institutions is critical to shaping India’s stance globally.17

The Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology has been a full-fledged ministry since 2016, before which it was a department in the Ministry of Communications and Information Technology. India’s Computer Emergency Response Team falls within the ministry.18 It serves as a nodal agency that addresses cybersecurity threats and co-ordinates with the emergency response teams of several other countries.

The MEA coordinates India’s cyber diplomacy and represents India at global cyber norms formulation fora (Ebert, Saslow and Wezling 2020, 11). It includes a cyber diplomacy division that represents India at multilateral technology fora, including the UN First Committee. The New and Emerging Strategic Technologies (NEST) division was set up to “engage in technology diplomacy and deal with the foreign policy and international legal aspects of new and emerging technologies” (Sabha 2020). The division will function under the supervision of a Head of Division to enable more active participation of India in global fora in the area of technology governance, and to promote the country’s national interests in that context (Sabha 2020), focusing on 5G and AI technologies (Chaudhury 2020). It will be critical to watch NEST’s functioning over the next few years, to see if it can shepherd a coherent approach to cyber norms formulation by drawing on the capacity and views of the other cybersecurity institutions. A co-ordinated approach among institutions was integral to India’s robust stances at previous negotiations—such as where the MEA and MOEF worked in tandem—to frame India’s stances at the UNFCCC.

Domestic Stakeholders (Non-governmental) and Political Interests

The lack of robust multistakeholder participation leading up to norms formulation processes has furthered India’s reticence. Several civil society groups have been active in raising issues with the framing of domestic technology policy on privacy, freedom of expression on the internet, net neutrality, and government surveillance. These issues have been debated at great lengths in parliament, courts and in academia (Sinha and Basu 2019). Presently, there is disagreement among multiple domestic camps on core security issues such as end-to-end encryption, data localisation, state surveillance, and the denial of internet access due to long-drawn internet shutdowns (Sinha and Basu 2019). However, relatively few scholars and organisations have published research or articulated public statements on India’s approach at global discussions covering cyber issues.19 There has also been limited media coverage of these negotiations with only a handful of news articles even referring to the ongoing First Committee processes—fewer than ten articles covering cyber norms negotiations being published in online or print media since 2017.20 Further, the articles that covered cyber norms processes were written to cover bilateral agreements between India and another country, rather than cover the negotiations themselves (‘Terrorism Poses Grave Threat’ 2017; India-Australia Affirm Commitment’ 2019). Furthermore, government engagement with civil society on the specific issue of cyber norms has been limited, although there is increasing engagement on other strategic issues, including the formulation of India’s national cybersecurity strategy.21 It is possible that NEST can foster multistakeholder collaboration and action in this regard, and seek expertise and input from experts to further India’s strategy going forward.

Similarly, industry lobbies perceive their interest in ensuring a robust cybersecurity posture, but they do not have clear positions on any fissures in the cyber norms debate. A number of Indian business guilds, such as the Federation of Indian Chambers of Commerce & Industry and the Internet and Mobile Association of India, signed up for the Paris Call on Trust and Security in Cyberspace to signal their willingness to co-operate with global cyber initiatives. While the present government has been responsive to industry lobbies in other debates, such as the formulation of India’s national cybersecurity strategy, it remains to be seen whether this vector will shape India’s behaviour at multilateral fora.

Will India Negotiate? Roadmap and Recommendations

It is incorrect to conclude that India’s passivity in the cyber norms space is due to a lack of comprehension or understanding of the significance of technology as a foreign policy priority. Current External Affairs Minister Jaishankar has underscored the significance of technology on multiple occasions.22 One does not need to look much further than India’s participation at multilateral fora on cross-border data flows to see how the hybrid intersection of strategic interests, institutional capacity and multistakeholder participation has produced robust and sustained engagement. India has consistently put forward reasoned and well-articulated positions across multilateral fora, constantly championing the needs of developing countries and the significance of ‘data sovereignty’ in the framing of digital policies (Basu 2019). On this issue, short-term interests are clearly outlined (Basu and Nachiappan 2020). The economic value of data as a resource and retaining control over data flows has been recognized by the government as part of its ‘Digital India’ push, which has led to a number of policy instruments mandating data localisation across sectors, while also constructing pivots for ensuring that the data of Indian citizens are used for India’s development (Basu and Nachiappan 2020).

The significance of short-term interests has spurred an efficient institutional approach with the Ministry of Commerce, which represents India at the WTO, and the MEA, working in conjunction with each other across multilateral fora. Several senior political leaders have put across clear views on ‘data colonialism’, or the extraction of economic gains by foreign technology companies, and the need to counter this practice (‘Companies shouldn’t be allowed’ 2019). Domestic interests shaping the policy landscape have also been more clearly defined, both by civil society actors and business lobbies, underpinning India’s stances on cross-border data flows at global trade fora.

It is expected that a policy shift at multilateral fora on cyber norms will emerge once the foreign policy establishment deems the First Committee negotiations to be a significant enough strategic priority, judiciously assesses domestic interests that may propel such a shift, and develops the institutional capacity to carry this shift through. India’s shift on the CTBT negotiations is a case in point, where the external environment caused policy-makers to undertake a carefully calibrated U-turn on signing the CTBT.

In the cyber norms debate, there are three factors that could shape a more assertive stance going forward. First, the strategic restrictions on Chinese technology indicates that the Indian government will utilise the opportunities and constraints presented by the geopolitical dimensions of technology. While India’s approach to the issue has been limited to restricting Chinese technology in India by citing provisions of Indian law and constitutional principles, it is clear that these restrictions can only serve as a short-term fix to a long-term threat from China. This is especially significant in light of recent reporting which indicates that China-linked actors may have carried out offensive cyber operations on electricity grids and other critical infrastructure in India (Sanger and Schmall 2021).

China has participated vigorously at multilateral fora, including norms formulation, standard-setting bodies, and other economic institutions, to ensure that the rules which govern global digital spaces reflect its principles and interests. China’s increased participation at global technology fora (Mueller 2020; Seaman 2020; Austin 2016, 171) might cause the Indian foreign policy establishment to take note and act. Rather than accepting rules forged by a geopolitical adversary, New Delhi may choose to accord more strategic significance to the multilateral fora, looking to formulate norms for responsible state behaviour in cyberspace. India has signalled some intent by signing onto the Global Partnership on AI and underscoring the spirit of ‘democratic resilience’ when shaping the rules of the road on critical and emerging technologies through the Quadrilateral Security Dialogue (‘The spirit of the Quad’ 2021). Membership in these select clubs is important, but not a replacement for participation at wider and more inclusive multilateral fora. India will have to carve out a space for itself in the functioning of these coalitions, while also shaping substantive outcomes at these processes, keeping inherent strategic interests at the forefront.

Second, this realization might serve as an impetus for the institutional ecosystem to take on a robust and coordinated approach. The emergence of new institutions last year, tasked with framing India’s strategic digital posture, in some way suggests institutional congruence may be possible. Further, any cyber doctrine will want to be in conjunction with international law and global norms in cyberspace, thus further incentivising the shaping of the rules and norms themselves. DCyA will be articulating a cyber doctrine, the NCSC has already prepared an updated cybersecurity strategy that should be published soon, and the MEA’s NEST might propel a diplomatic posture that works with other institutions in the ecosystem (Ray and Dei 2020).

Finally, civil society and academia are increasingly taking cybersecurity and its geopolitical concerns as an important public issue. Both newspapers and television channels have covered the technological dimensions of the China conflict, which means that the security dimensions of technology are playing a more significant role in civil discourse. Greater civic discourse might propel the foreign policy establishment to articulate publicly the role it has played in driving global discourse on these issues. The key question is whether the Indian government and interested actors will view the First Committee processes as the avenue of asserting these interests, or continue with the other approaches it has adopted thus far. Importantly, if civil society organisations do not view these negotiations as significant or are unable to influence India’s role in the negotiations, then institutional capacity within the government would need to step up and play a bigger role in carrying its interests forward.

Conclusion

The hybridity of India’s engagement with cyber norms formulation processes has important implications on calibrating expectations from other rising powers and understanding their engagement with these processes. First, it pays to look beyond voting positions or statements made at multilateral processes, and explore the intersections between national interests, institutional capacity, domestic interests, and engagement with other technology regimes. Critically, relinquishing domestic control over the internet could precipitate either diffident or hostile attitudes toward multilateral negotiations covering cyber issues, given the uncertainties around the domestic implications of international rule-making for developing countries. Engagement, however, is not just a factor of capacity, but of several other institutional and political factors, as this chapter has outlined. Second, the tag of a ‘digital decider’ or ‘swing states’ seldom helps evaluate or predict how countries like India, or indeed similarly positioned countries such as Brazil or Indonesia, might engage with or affect cyber norms debates. Given the complexity of domestic politics and international interests, their engagement should be pegged to understanding the unique politics and architecture of each country rather than analysing and pontificating whether their external behaviour comports with US or Russian visions of the internet. This means that when countries like India adopt positions on contentious issues in the cyber norms debates, such as the applicability of international law, they are likely to be delinked from prior interpretations put forward by the Tallinn Manual or even by other states thus far.

In a multipolar world, India is unlikely to make cyber rules. A more desirable goal would be for India to function as a rule shaper, working with developed and developing countries to influence global cyber rules and norms. This scenario would necessarily draw on India’s economic strength and diplomatic acumen, which have shaped a panoply of international rules over the years. However, while we firmly believe that it is in India’s strategic interest to function as a rule shaper, three key factors will determine the extent and scope of India’s negotiating strategy: interests (economic and security), institutional capacity, and domestic preferences of key stakeholders. These factors have shaped India’s negotiating strategies.

That said, three emergent factors may alter India’s passivity vis-a-vis cyber norms. First, immediate geopolitical tensions with China may enhance the need to shape global rules and norms to counter Chinese influence on global technology issues. Second, several institutions are being set up across ministries which indicates both an increase in capacity and a willingness to set up a cyber doctrine. Finally, due to increased public discourse, the government may start responding more to the expertise and interests of various stake-holders, failing which the burden of shaping India’s stance will fall on state institutions.

It is in India’s strategic interest to play a more active role in this space, burnish its reputation globally and avoid taking rules designed by other countries. Given continuing geopolitical fissures and uncertainty over the future of a rules-based order in cyberspace, several external stakeholders are waiting for India to take a stand that is reflective of its rich constitutional ethos and democratic values. However, we must be cognizant of the hybrid constraints and interests that have driven and are likely to drive India’s strategy going forward.
